# Changelog

## Unreleased

### Breaking

- A condition of `cond` must be `true` or `false`, as a filter must, and `else` always holds unless a scope declares it. (`cond-empty-string-condition` is `error`, was `false`; `cond-null-condition` is `error`, was `false`; `cond-string-condition` is `error`, was `true`; `cond-zero-condition` is `error`, was `false`)
- A `define` binds in the body it stands in, a lambda's or the program's, and a name read before its `define` has run fails with `UndefinedYetError`. (`define-inside-lambda` is `local`, was `global`; `host-callback-define` is `local`, was `global`)
- Each string has a `meaning`: a `name` or a `binding`, with the binding it means or makes (a `parameter`, a `define` in a lambda's body or at the top, a `relation`, a `schema`, or the `environment`'s, with the pointers of the strings that bind it), a `logic-variable`, `data`, `unbound` where only a name can stand and nothing binds one, a `form`'s name, or the `keyword` `else`. (`analyze-string` is `read-by-scope`, was `reference-candidate`)

### Added

- How the engine follows a change moved from the README to `packages/core/src/logic/ENGINE.md`. (`engine-md` added)
- An array that names a form but does not have the form's shape fails with `MalformedFormError`, which says what the form takes, with an example. (`MalformedFormError` added)
- A `define` binds in the body it stands in, a lambda's or the program's, and a name read before its `define` has run fails with `UndefinedYetError`. (`UndefinedYetError` added; `setLocalBinding` added)
- A new form, `set!`, changes the nearest binding of a name, as in Scheme. (`set!` added)
- Each string has a `meaning`: a `name` or a `binding`, with the binding it means or makes (a `parameter`, a `define` in a lambda's body or at the top, a `relation`, a `schema`, or the `environment`'s, with the pointers of the strings that bind it), a `logic-variable`, `data`, `unbound` where only a name can stand and nothing binds one, a `form`'s name, or the `keyword` `else`. (`analysis-string-meaning` added)
- `analyze(expression, environment)` reads a program as a run with that environment reads it, by the same scopes, found by the functions the evaluator finds them by: `definedIn`, `declaredIn`, and `bodyNames`, with `nameOrString` for a misspelled name. (`analyze-environment` added)
- The graph editor analyzes a program with the environment its runs bind and shows under each string what it means there; selecting a name or a binding outlines the binding and every string that means it. (`editor-string-meanings` added)
- The run on formal verification (2026-11-19) asks what Lion should add so a model can be proved correct, the way Alchemy's Verify proves its domain models with the Z3 solver. It weighs ten ideas against twelve programs on a bank account, in `research/probes/fv-*.json`, which show that Lion keeps invariants at every change and can search every sequence of commands up to a bound in plain code, but refuses any question about every value a variable could take. It ranks first a solver for the values a question leaves open, then bounded search with inputs drawn from schemas, then rules about what must eventually happen; proofs that every command keeps the invariants, invariant inference, and proof certificates build on the solver. (`formal-verification-research` added)
- The research model now puts every run's ideas in one order. The rules that ordered the formal-verification ideas moved into the shared frame, and new rules say which ideas are left to build, which can start now and which wait for another, which would change a rule Lion documents, and why each other idea was passed over. Its page, `research/runs/consolidation/`, ranks first a solver for the values a question leaves open, which would change the rule that every variable in a filter must first be bound, so it waits for a decision. Every program earlier runs kept was run again on Lion as it is now: an aggregated relation now takes facts, and a condition must be true or false, so one program now asks `value/number?`. (`research-consolidation` added)
- The run on TufaZip (2026-11-19) asks what Lion can learn from a compressor whose decoder learns in step with its encoder and whose benchmark counts the decoder in the score. Lion already makes a change pay for what it adds, as `guidelines/measuring.md` asks, and rebuilds its state from steps in any order. It adds one idea, a held-out eval case for the skills' improvement loop, because a score tuned on one fixed case can improve without the skill improving. A Lion program, `research/probes/tz-figures.json`, works out eleven of the page's figures from its table, and all of them hold. (`tufazip-research` added)
- schema/arrayOf takes the most items a list may have as a second argument, and then lists every such list. (`schema/arrayOf-most` added)
- A schema lists its values only where no relation goal, '=', or 'in' can bind the value first, and at most 100,000 of them. (`schemas-listing-their-values` added)
- Every goal that waits only for such variables joins one step that runs after the goals its other variables need, and the solver decides it (`solving-typed-variables` added)
- array/some? and array/every? say whether a function holds for some item and for each, stopping at the first that settles it, as Racket's ormap and andmap do. (`array/every?` added; `array/some?` added)
- array/reductions gives every value array/reduce passes through, starting with the first, so a step function gives the states of a run (`array/reductions` added)
- The solver runs array functions on a list of known length, such as one ['schema/arrayOf', schema, most] stands for, item by item, and in takes each of its items, so a check of every run of commands leaves the amounts the commands carry to the solver and covers every amount. (`solving-runs-of-commands` added)
- ['schema/includes?', a, b] says whether every value a allows fits b, decided exactly, by listing a's values or by the solver, and refused by name otherwise. (`schema/includes?` added)
- Where the solver's step stopped a rule, why shows a certificate for each case the solver split the goals into (`solver-proof-certificates` added)
- The solver runs a lambda written where it runs, closed over the values it works out, by its body (`solver-runs-closures` added)

### Removed

- The timeline no longer counts commits, and lists the sources of an item in the order its commits and notes were made, then the documents, then what Lion does today, so the pages a working tree gives are the pages its commit holds. (`changelog-commit-count` removed)
- The `relationships` field, with its `reference-candidate` and `definition` kinds, is gone. (`analysis-relationships` removed)

### Changed

- A fact that holds an unknown is followed through every group that invents, and every invariant is asked after it. (`fact-holding-unknown-after-change` is `followed`, was `not-followed`)
- Every group of relations is followed row by row, whatever its rules look like, except the two kinds whose reads are not known: one whose rule holds a function from the host, and one whose rule reads a changed relation through a schema. (`invention-joined-otherwise-after-fact-change` is `followed`, was `derived-again`)
- Two values a key or an equality rule makes one are a pair, which is a row with candidates too, and a row that holds a value its class no longer writes ends at the level the pair is held from, is kept beside the rows that hold, and gives the row it became a candidate. (`merged-unknowns-after-retract` is `apart-again`)
- A sum or a mean of numbers that are not all whole is added up in sort order. (`rule-head-sum-of-fractions` is `added-in-sort-order`, was `added-in-order-found`)
- A row that comes while another row with its key is held is read from the level after: by then the pair that makes the two one holds, and the row that holds the value its class no longer writes has ended, so no answer finds two rows with one key. (`rule-reading-two-rows-with-one-key-in-its-group` is `never-both`)
- `why` finds how a row came about when it is asked: of the answers that give the row at the level it is held from, the least of the first rule that has one, worked out from the rows there are. (`why-of-a-row-several-answers-give` is `least-at-its-level`)
- A claim that quotes a document in words the document no longer holds is left out of the model, and `bun run history check` lists it as a note instead of failing. (`history-check-on-reworded-doc-sentence` is `note`, was `fails`)
- A condition of `cond` must be `true` or `false`, as a filter must, and `else` always holds unless a scope declares it. (`cond-else` is `holds-unless-declared`, was `ordinary-string`)
- A parameter or a `define` can take a special form's name, and an array that starts with it is a call where the binding is in scope; a relation or schema still cannot, since its name is bound in every run of its database. (`define-special-form-name` is `accepted`, was `rejected`; `lambda-special-form-name-parameter` is `accepted`, was `rejected`)
- A relation whose rules aggregate merges what each key gets with those aggregates, as one that declares a merge does: the other columns of the head are the key, and every fact of the relation and every rule with a plain head for it gives a candidate, whatever order they come in. (`aggregated-relation-facts` is `candidate`, was `error`; `aggregated-relation-plain-rule` is `candidate`, was `error`)
- A `rule` or `invariant` form inside `retract`, written as it was added, takes that rule or invariant back, and the relations whose rules changed are derived again. (`invariant-removal` is `possible`, was `impossible`; `rule-removal` is `possible`, was `impossible`)
- A merge takes any aggregate, `aggregate/sum` and `aggregate/count` among them; only a lattice aggregate can go through recursion. (`merge-with-sum` is `sum`, was `error`)
- No invariant refuses an assumed change, not even the one an assumed rule that says two values are the same holds as, which the same rule in the database does not hold either for an assumed fact. (`assuming-equality-rule-invariant` is `not-kept`)
- In a rule, the variables of a held rule that the rule around it binds take the answer's values, and every other one is the held rule's own, as for every question. (`assuming-held-rule-outer-variables` is `bound`)
- A rule or invariant form there is held, never made: its parts are evaluated as the form would evaluate them, and the database does not change, also when the question is refused. (`assuming-refused-change-database` is `unchanged`, was `rule-added`)
- A change of `assuming` is a `fact`, `retract`, or `rule` form, made as that form makes it on its own: `["assuming", ["rule", head, ...body], expression]` adds the rule, and a `retract` takes back the rules and invariants it names as well as facts, so `["assuming", ["retract", ["rule", head, ...body]], expression]` asks what would hold without the rule. (`assuming-rule-change` is `possible`, was `error`; `assuming-rule-retraction` is `possible`, was `error`)
- A `retract` of facts and rules is one change, and a rule that asks `assuming` is derived again and compared after a change, as one that holds a function from the host is. (`retract-rule-beside-facts-refused` is `no-change`, was `facts-removed`; `rule-asking-assuming-after-fact-change` is `up-to-date`, was `stale`)
- A `fact` or `retract` change of `assuming` states rows with `exists` as the form does on its own: `["assuming", ["fact", ["exists", ["?m"], ["and", ["road", "c", "?m"], ["road", "?m", "e"]]]], ["reaches", "a", "e"]]` asks what would hold with a road through a town nobody has named. Each change has its own form's shape, checked as that form checks it, wherever it stands among the changes. (`assuming-exists-change` is `possible`, was `error`)
- The first element of an array names what the array does, so a string there is a name and never data: one that no scope declares is looked up where the call runs, among the bindings the run has by then. A relation or function that code `eval` ran has bound is called, in plain code and in a rule, query, or `find` alike: after `["eval", ["quote", ["relation", "visited", ["city"]]]]`, `["find", "?c", ["visited", "?c"]]` asks the relation, while the string `"visited"` anywhere else is data. (`eval-declared-relation-asked-later` is `asked`, was `error`)
- `aggregate/sum` gives the number nearest the exact sum of its values, rounded once, as Python's `math.fsum` does, so the order the values come in never changes it: `["aggregate/sum", ["array/make", 1e16, 1, -1e16]]` is 1 in any order. `aggregate/mean` divides that sum. A rule that aggregates, a merge of the candidates of a key, and a call on a list give the same sum for the same values. It fails with `ArithmeticError` only when the exact sum is beyond every number, also where adding in order would pass the largest number on the way: `[1e308, 1e308, -1e308]` sums to 1e308. (`sum-exactly-rounded` is `nearest`, was `accumulated`)
- The analysis that the graph editor shows reads the head of a call as the evaluator does: a string there that no scope declares is `unbound`, a name nothing binds where it is written, and not data. (`analysis-call-head` is `unbound`, was `data`)
- When nothing else binds the value schema/fits? checks, and its schema can list the values it allows, the goal binds the value to each of them that fits, as in binds its element to each item of a list. (`schema-lists-its-values` is `lists`, was `error`)
- A variable that a schema/fits? types with a schema that says what kind its values are, such as value/integer? or a record of such schemas, and that no relation goal, '=', or 'in' binds, stands for every value the schema allows. (`filter-typed-variable` is `solved`, was `error`)
- A goal the solver cannot decide, such as a product of two unknowns, a string function, or a question, waits for values (`solver-waiting-goal` is `solved`)
- A find gives one value for every match of the variables the solver works out, as it does for values a schema lists, so a variable its template leaves out still makes its own matches: with ?x from -2 to 2, a find of a cond on ?x gives "other" three times and "pos" twice. (`find-solved-variable-per-match` is `per-match`, was `per-template-value`)
- A rule that aggregates combines one value from each match of the variables the solver works out, as it does for values a schema lists. (`rule-aggregate-solved-variable-per-match` is `per-match`, was `per-head-value`)
- A variable that a goal about the solver's values defines with cond takes the value of each case that can hold, not only of the first the solver finds. (`solved-defined-variable-each-case` is `every-case`, was `one-case`)
- A schema computed where it is written, such as one that holds a lambda or one a function is given, stands for its values as the same schema under a name does: listed when it can list them, solved for otherwise. (`computed-schema-solved-for` is `solved`, was `error`)
- why now explains a row the solver gave with the row's own values. (`why-solved-row-values` is `row-values`, was `other-values`)
- ['schema/includes?', a, b] says whether every value a allows fits b (`schema-includes-integer-in-number` is `included`)
- and runs a library function only with as many arguments as the library says it takes (`solver-library-call-wrong-arguments` is `error`, was `run`)

### Fixed

- An array that names a form but does not have the form's shape fails with `MalformedFormError`, which says what the form takes, with an example. (`malformed-special-form` is `says-what-the-form-takes`, was `not-a-call`)
- A body that defines the name of a form it uses fails, as R7RS Scheme makes such a definition an error: `["define", "define", 1]` calls the name it defines, which the body declares from its start and which has no value yet. It fails with `UndefinedYetError`, whose message says that the body defines the form's name, so an array that starts with it calls that definition instead of being the form, and that the definition needs another name. (`body-defines-define` is `says-why`, was `read-before-defined`)
- The parts of a logic form are read by the scope the form is written in, as plain code is: a `find`, a `rule`, a `fact`, a `query`, an `assuming`, and every other form that reads or changes the database compiles its parts there, so a string means in them what it means in plain code beside them. A scope keeps the names it declared when code was compiled in it: a name that code `eval` runs binds later is not among them, also when compiled code asks its scope while it runs, as a form that waits does when it keeps the values of the names it reads. Code that `eval` runs is a program: it declares the names it defines, in the body `eval` stands in, and the relations and schemas it declares, from its start. The first element of an array names what the array does, so a string there is a name and never data: one that no scope declares is looked up where the call runs, among the bindings the run has by then. A relation or function that code `eval` ran has bound is called, in plain code and in a rule, query, or `find` alike: after `["eval", ["quote", ["relation", "visited", ["city"]]]]`, `["find", "?c", ["visited", "?c"]]` asks the relation, while the string `"visited"` anywhere else is data. (`eval-code-own-definitions` is `declared`, was `error`; `string-in-find-bound-after-compile` is `data`, was `name`)
- A `fact` or `why` form that holds a `rule` or `invariant` form among its relation calls fails before it evaluates any of them, so it adds no rule: `["fact", ["n", 1], ["rule", ["m", "?x"], ["n", "?x"]]]` fails and the database holds no new rule or fact. `retract` and `assuming` check a rule or invariant form they hold as the form checks itself, in the same order and words. (`fact-holding-rule-form` is `changes-nothing`, was `adds-the-rule`)
- The property that a rule or invariant form is refused in retract and assuming as the form itself is refused now leaves out an invariant that is well formed but does not hold, such as `["invariant", ["quote", true], {"assert": "none"}]`: adding it is refused when it is checked, not as a form, and retracting it removes nothing, which the property now checks. It failed within about 1,000 runs in each of three passes of 5,000, about one test run in five at its 200 runs, and holds in three passes of 5,000 now. (`retract-property-false-invariant` is `retract-removes-nothing`, was `expected-refused`)
- A form that waits reads a logic variable's value once the variable is bound, also where it runs into a form that waits again: in `["rule", ["out", "?x", ["cond", [["number/equals?", "?x", 0], "?x"], ["else", ["find", ["number/add", "?k", ["cond", [["even", "?x"], "?x"], ["else", "?x"]]], ["link", "?x", "?k"]]]]], ["n", "?x"]]`, the `cond` that asks `["even", "?x"]` inside the `find`'s template asks it of the value `?x` has, and the rule gives what plain code gives with `?x` bound. It failed with `Expected number`, because that `cond` took `?x` for a new variable. (`waiting-form-waits-again` is `bound-value`, was `error`)

### Decided

- Every derived row has a weight: the number of its candidates, each a fact or an answer of a rule's body that gives the row, and the row holds while its weight is above zero. (`following-a-change-to-facts`: `candidates-by-level` over `take-out-and-derive-again`, `weights-alone`, `rounds-in-a-trace`)
- A sum or a mean of numbers that are not all whole is added up in sort order. (`order-a-rule-head-sum-adds-in`: `sort-order` over `order-found`)
- The answers of a rule that invents are rows of a relation of their own, and each is asked at the end of the stage it is first held in whether a row held by then gives its head. (`rule-that-invents-on-change`: `weighted-rows` over `follow-a-component-at-a-time`, `derive-again-every-case`, `skolem-for-keyed-relations`, was `follow-where-it-invents-alone`)
- A row that comes while another row with its key is held is read from the level after: by then the pair that makes the two one holds, and the row that holds the value its class no longer writes has ended, so no answer finds two rows with one key. (`two-rows-with-one-key-inside-a-group`: `read-from-the-level-after` over `both-for-a-level`, `pair-from-the-same-level`)
- A group whose rules read nothing of its own keeps a weight for each row in its table and nothing else, and any other group keeps a row's candidates and time on the row itself. (`what-a-group-keeps-of-its-rows`: `what-its-rules-need` over `an-entry-for-every-row`)
- `why` finds how a row came about when it is asked: of the answers that give the row at the level it is held from, the least of the first rule that has one, worked out from the rows there are. (`where-why-finds-a-derivation`: `found-when-asked` over `kept-with-each-row`, `derived-again-after-a-change`)
- A screen recording is the proof of a change people see, such as the graph editor or the website; a change to the language, the CLI, the tools, or the docs' text is proved by its properties, `bun run compare`, and its measurements. (`completion-proof-scope`: `visible-changes` over `every-change`)
- How the engine follows a change moved from the README to `packages/core/src/logic/ENGINE.md`. (`engine-mechanics-docs`: `engine-md` over `readme-guarantees`)
- The timeline no longer counts commits, and lists the sources of an item in the order its commits and notes were made, then the documents, then what Lion does today, so the pages a working tree gives are the pages its commit holds. (`record-pages-and-their-commit`: `pages-from-the-record-alone` over `second-pages-commit`, `pages-built-in-ci`)
- A claim that quotes a document in words the document no longer holds is left out of the model, and `bun run history check` lists it as a note instead of failing. (`reworded-doc-sentence`: `claim-left-out` over `check-fails`)
- A condition of `cond` must be `true` or `false`, as a filter must, and `else` always holds unless a scope declares it. (`condition-truth`: `true-or-false` over `javascript-truthiness`, `scheme-truth`)
- A `define` binds in the body it stands in, a lambda's or the program's, and a name read before its `define` has run fails with `UndefinedYetError`. (`define-scope`: `body` over `global`)
- A parameter or a `define` can take a special form's name, and an array that starts with it is a call where the binding is in scope; a relation or schema still cannot, since its name is bound in every run of its database. (`form-name-bindings`: `shadow-in-scope` over `refused`)
- A string is a name when a scope it is written in declares it, and data otherwise, settled before it runs, so a binding made later never turns data into a name. (`unbound-string-semantics`: `settled-by-scope` over `keep-string-fallback`, `ref-form`, `call-form`, was `keep-string-fallback`)
- A relation whose rules aggregate merges what each key gets with those aggregates, as one that declares a merge does: the other columns of the head are the key, and every fact of the relation and every rule with a plain head for it gives a candidate, whatever order they come in. (`aggregated-relation-candidates`: `merge-from-rules` over `facts-refused`)
- A merge takes any aggregate, `aggregate/sum` and `aggregate/count` among them; only a lattice aggregate can go through recursion. (`merging-what-a-key-gets`: `any-aggregate-merge` over `lattice-merge-option`, `written-merge-function`, `merge-on-write`, was `lattice-merge-option`)
- A `rule` or `invariant` form inside `retract`, written as it was added, takes that rule or invariant back, and the relations whose rules changed are derived again. (`rule-retraction`: `retract-the-form` over `impossible`, `by-name`)
- A change of `assuming` is a `fact`, `retract`, or `rule` form, made as that form makes it on its own: `["assuming", ["rule", head, ...body], expression]` adds the rule, and a `retract` takes back the rules and invariants it names as well as facts, so `["assuming", ["retract", ["rule", head, ...body]], expression]` asks what would hold without the rule. (`assuming-changes`: `fact-retract-rule` over `fact-retract-only`, `invariant-changes`)
- In a rule, the variables of a held rule that the rule around it binds take the answer's values, and every other one is the held rule's own, as for every question. (`held-rule-variables`: `question-scope` over `held-rule-own`)
- `analyze(expression, environment)` reads a program as a run with that environment reads it, by the same scopes, found by the functions the evaluator finds them by: `definedIn`, `declaredIn`, and `bodyNames`, with `nameOrString` for a misspelled name. (`analysis-strings`: `read-by-scope` over `reference-candidate`, `read-by-running`)
- The first element of an array names what the array does, so a string there is a name and never data: one that no scope declares is looked up where the call runs, among the bindings the run has by then. A relation or function that code `eval` ran has bound is called, in plain code and in a rule, query, or `find` alike: after `["eval", ["quote", ["relation", "visited", ["city"]]]]`, `["find", "?c", ["visited", "?c"]]` asks the relation, while the string `"visited"` anywhere else is data. (`call-head-name`: `name-looked-up-where-the-call-runs` over `settled-by-scope-as-data`, `database-names-anywhere`)
- The parts of a logic form are read by the scope the form is written in, as plain code is: a `find`, a `rule`, a `fact`, a `query`, an `assuming`, and every other form that reads or changes the database compiles its parts there, so a string means in them what it means in plain code beside them. A scope keeps the names it declared when code was compiled in it: a name that code `eval` runs binds later is not among them, also when compiled code asks its scope while it runs, as a form that waits does when it keeps the values of the names it reads. Code that `eval` runs is a program: it declares the names it defines, in the body `eval` stands in, and the relations and schemas it declares, from its start. The first element of an array names what the array does, so a string there is a name and never data: one that no scope declares is looked up where the call runs, among the bindings the run has by then. A relation or function that code `eval` ran has bound is called, in plain code and in a rule, query, or `find` alike: after `["eval", ["quote", ["relation", "visited", ["city"]]]]`, `["find", "?c", ["visited", "?c"]]` asks the relation, while the string `"visited"` anywhere else is data. (`eval-code-declarations`: `declares-its-definitions` over `compiled-as-is`; `logic-form-strings`: `written-scope` over `running-scope`)
- A body that defines the name of a form it uses fails, as R7RS Scheme makes such a definition an error: `["define", "define", 1]` calls the name it defines, which the body declares from its start and which has no value yet. It fails with `UndefinedYetError`, whose message says that the body defines the form's name, so an array that starts with it calls that definition instead of being the form, and that the definition needs another name. (`form-name-definition-in-own-body`: `error-that-says-why` over `definition-stays-the-form`)
- `aggregate/sum` gives the number nearest the exact sum of its values, rounded once, as Python's `math.fsum` does, so the order the values come in never changes it: `["aggregate/sum", ["array/make", 1e16, 1, -1e16]]` is 1 in any order. `aggregate/mean` divides that sum. A rule that aggregates, a merge of the candidates of a key, and a call on a list give the same sum for the same values. It fails with `ArithmeticError` only when the exact sum is beyond every number, also where adding in order would pass the largest number on the way: `[1e308, 1e308, -1e308]` sums to 1e308. (`sum-rounding`: `nearest-exact-sum` over `added-as-values-come`, `added-in-sorted-order`)
- When nothing else binds the value schema/fits? checks, and its schema can list the values it allows, the goal binds the value to each of them that fits, as in binds its element to each item of a list. (`schema-binds-its-value`: `list-finite-schemas` over `schema-values-function`, `size-bounded-listing`, `written-order`)
- The solver is Lion's own, with exact rationals: Fourier and Motzkin's method for numbers that may be any number, Pugh's Omega test for whole numbers. (`solve-typed-variables`: `exact-linear-decision-in-lion` over `z3-in-webassembly`, `formula-terms`, `bounded-labelling`, `real-relaxation`, `limited-listing`)
- A rule that must always hold is a question that no state of any run breaks it, one that must eventually hold a question that some state of every run meets it, and one checked at the end of every run a question about what array/reduce gives. (`eventually-rules`: `questions-over-run-states` over `temporal-forms`, `deferred-invariants`, `bounded-recursive-rules`)
- A proof that a command keeps a rule is a not over an exists of a typed state the rule allows, a typed command, and a next state that breaks the rule, with no new form (`command-proofs`: `induction-as-a-question` over `proof-form`, `database-state-proofs`, `outside-certificates`)

### Rules

- The timeline no longer counts commits, and lists the sources of an item in the order its commits and notes were made, then the documents, then what Lion does today, so the pages a working tree gives are the pages its commit holds. (rule `pages-from-the-record-alone`)
- A condition of `cond` must be `true` or `false`, as a filter must, and `else` always holds unless a scope declares it. (rule `cond-truthiness` withdrawn)
- The parts of a logic form are read by the scope the form is written in, as plain code is: a `find`, a `rule`, a `fact`, a `query`, an `assuming`, and every other form that reads or changes the database compiles its parts there, so a string means in them what it means in plain code beside them. A scope keeps the names it declared when code was compiled in it: a name that code `eval` runs binds later is not among them, also when compiled code asks its scope while it runs, as a form that waits does when it keeps the values of the names it reads. Code that `eval` runs is a program: it declares the names it defines, in the body `eval` stands in, and the relations and schemas it declares, from its start. The first element of an array names what the array does, so a string there is a name and never data: one that no scope declares is looked up where the call runs, among the bindings the run has by then. A relation or function that code `eval` ran has bound is called, in plain code and in a rule, query, or `find` alike: after `["eval", ["quote", ["relation", "visited", ["city"]]]]`, `["find", "?c", ["visited", "?c"]]` asks the relation, while the string `"visited"` anywhere else is data. (rule `strings-settled-by-scope`)
- `analyze(expression, environment)` reads a program as a run with that environment reads it, by the same scopes, found by the functions the evaluator finds them by: `definedIn`, `declaredIn`, and `bodyNames`, with `nameOrString` for a misspelled name. (rule `analysis-reads-as-evaluator`)
- When nothing else binds the value schema/fits? checks, and its schema can list the values it allows, the goal binds the value to each of them that fits, as in binds its element to each item of a list. (rule `schema-binds-its-value`)
- Variables nothing needs only need some values to exist, so exists asks whether one does and not proves that none does, for every value, and why explains a row with an example. (rule `solved-questions`)

## 2026-11-18 — @lionlang/core 0.14.1, @lionlang/cli 0.13.1

No invariant refuses a change that `assuming` only assumes: an invariant keeps the database, and an assumed change does not change it. In 0.14.0 an invariant whose filter asked with `assuming` could hold of different facts depending on whether it was declared before them or after, and one whose `assuming` added a new fact for each answer never finished. Now it holds of the same facts in any order and never asks itself again.

### Changed

- With `["invariant", ["shift", "?p", "sun"], {"assert": "none"}]`, adding `["fact", ["shift", "cy", "sun"]]` fails, while `["assuming", ["fact", ["shift", "cy", "sun"]], ["find", "?p", ["shift", "?p", "sun"]]]` is `["cy"]`. (`assuming-change-an-invariant-refuses` is `assumed`, was `error`)
- An invariant can ask with `assuming` what a change would do, holds of the same facts whatever order they and the invariant came in, and never asks itself again. (`invariant-asking-assuming-order` is `independent`, was `depends-on-order`; `invariant-assuming-a-new-fact-each-answer` is `ends`, was `does-not-end`)

### Decided

- `assuming` makes its changes with keys and merges, and no invariant refuses them: an invariant keeps the database, and a change that is only assumed does not change the database. (`assuming-and-invariants`: `invariants-keep-the-database` over `checked-like-fact`, `skipped-inside-invariants`)

## 2026-11-18 — @lionlang/core 0.14.0, @lionlang/cli 0.13.0

Plain code is compiled to JavaScript functions and runs 9 to 25 times faster, and a call in tail position keeps nothing of the call it is in, so a loop runs in constant memory. `["assuming", change, ..., expression]` asks what would hold after a change and leaves the database as it is. `schema/open` names the rest of a record or list, and a keyed relation can declare `"merge"`, a lattice aggregate that joins the values its key gets. When a key or an equality rule makes values one, the value kept is their least, so answers no longer depend on the order of rules or facts; this is a breaking change, as is host code that read a scope's bindings through a `Ref`. A group whose rules invent unknowns follows a change to facts one component of values at a time, and a schema whose lambda asks about itself no longer runs Lion out of memory. Research runs share one Lion model in `research/`.

### Breaking

- When a key or an equality rule makes values one, the value kept is their least: a given value, else the unknown that nests the fewest unknowns, else the one that sorts first. (`equality-rule-two-invented-unknowns` is `fewest-nested-then-first-sorted`, was `first-sorted`)
- Host code that read a scope through `Ref.get(environment.bindingsRef)` reads `environment.bindings`, a `Map`, or calls `lookup` (`environment-bindings-read-by-host` is `map`, was `ref`)

### Added

- What Lion does is a claim quoting a sentence of its docs or the output of a program in `research/probes/`, saved as a source by `research/probes/run.mjs`, and each conclusion is derived by a rule. (`research-probes-runner` added)
- `guidelines/researching.md` states the practice, `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md` name it, and the skill's `SKILL.md` says to add a run to a model the project already keeps. (`researching-guideline` added)
- The separation-logic run is the first run in `research/`. (`separation-logic-research` added)
- An expression is compiled to a JavaScript function of the scope it runs in (`compile` added)
- `schema/open` takes one or two arguments, and a list of schemas as well as a record. (`schema-open-rest` added)
- Each schema a library function checks its arguments with is compiled, once, into a guard (`guardOf` added)
- A keyed relation can declare `"merge"`: for columns outside the key, the lattice aggregate that joins the values a key gets, `aggregate/min`, `aggregate/max`, `aggregate/shortest`, or `aggregate/union`. (`relation-merge-option` added)
- A frozen eval of what a record of observations is worth when re-observing is expensive and nothing else says what changed. It runs in two sessions, and between them the project changes without the agent being told. (`reasoning-eval-rechecks-what-changed` added)
- `--bare` runs the case with no skill at all. (`skill-eval-bare` added)
- `--from <folder>` runs a changed copy of the skill instead of the one in `skills/`, or another skill to compare it with; the agent is told the name its `SKILL.md` gives. (`skill-eval-from-other-skill` added)
- A case may run its agent several times in the same folder, as work that a later session picks up. Its prompts are then a `sessions/` folder of files named by number, `1.md`, `2.md`, run in that order, and each later prompt gets the message the session before it ended with wherever it says `{{earlier}}`. (`skill-eval-sessions` added)
- A case's `setup.mjs` makes the world around the sessions. It may export `start()`, called once before the runs, which returns what to `stop()` after them, such as a service the agents call; `prepare(work)`, which lays out a run's folder before its first session; `between(work, finished)`, called after each session but the last, for the changes the next session is not told about; and `finish(work, logs)`, which may leave a record beside the run's transcripts. (`skill-eval-setup` added)
- `["assuming", change, ..., expression]` gives what the expression gives in the database as it would be after the changes, each a `fact` or `retract` form made in order as that form makes it, with keys, merges, and invariants, and leaves the database as it is. (`assuming` added)
- The run on what to improve in Lion next (2026-11-17) is the second run in the shared research model: its sources, its 275 quoted claims, its directions as candidates, and the two programs that model invented unknowns and annotations are in `research/` beside the separation-logic run, under the names that run already used, and its ranking comes out the same there. (`lion-improvements-research` added)
- The run on CIRT, the calculus of inductive refinement types (arXiv 2611.20867, 2026-11-18), is the third run in the shared research model. It weighs seven ideas from the paper against Lion's schemas and rules, and ranks first stratified recursion for schemas: a schema may ask about itself only about a smaller part of the value. Its fourteen programs, in `research/probes/ci-*.json`, show that a schema whose predicate asks about itself, about the same value, runs Lion out of memory, before and after compiled evaluation. (`cirt-refinement-types-research` added)

### Removed

- `getBinding` and `setLocalBinding` are gone (`getBinding` removed; `setLocalBinding` removed)

### Changed

- When a key or an equality rule makes values one, the value kept is their least: a given value, else the unknown that nests the fewest unknowns, else the one that sorts first. It used to depend on the order the values were made one, so swapping two equality rules could change every unknown a program showed: with ben's and cy's invented mothers and cy's grandmother made one by two equality rules, one order kept cy's mother and the other ben's. Now both keep ben's mother, which nests no unknown and sorts before cy's. (`equality-rules-order` is `independent`, was `depends-on-order`)
- the unknown takes the other row's value: a given value, or, when both are such unknowns, the one that nests fewer unknowns, and of those the one that sorts first (`keyed-rows-two-invented-unknowns` is `fewest-nested-then-first-sorted`)
- `guidelines/researching.md` states the practice, `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md` name it, and the skill's `SKILL.md` says to add a run to a model the project already keeps. (`research-extends-existing-model` is `true`)
- A call in tail position keeps nothing of the call it is in, so a loop of 1,000,000 tail calls takes 0.5 s and 9 MB, and a call that is not in tail position can still nest as deep as memory allows. (`deep-call-not-in-tail-position` is `works`; `tail-call-loop-memory` is `constant`, was `grows-with-calls`)
- A key the record names is never part of the rest, so `["schema/open", a, b]` of two records of schemas with no key in common is the one exact schema with the keys of both, and a variable as the rest binds the rest in a pattern. (`schema-open-composes-exact-schemas` is `fits`; `schema-open-rest-binds` is `the-other-keys`)
- What it leaves over is the rest: the record of the other keys, or the list of the items after. (`schema-open-list-rest` is `the-other-items`)
- Every group of relations is followed row by row, whatever its rules look like, except the two kinds whose reads are not known: one whose rule holds a function from the host, and one whose rule reads a changed relation through a schema. (`equality-rule-group-after-fact-change` is `followed`; `keyed-invention-after-fact-change` is `followed`)
- A fact of such a relation never replaces another, and `retract` takes it back. (`merge-fact-retract` is `candidate-removed`)
- Every row a key gets is then a candidate, from a fact or a rule alike, and the key holds one row whose merged columns join the candidates' values. (`merge-joins-facts-and-rules` is `join`)
- It pays most where what you observed is expensive to observe again, such as an eval, a benchmark, or a slow run, and nothing else says which files changed since: uncommitted work in the files, a shared working tree, a stash, a folder outside git. (`reasoning-skill-when-it-pays` is `expensive-observations-undated-changes`)
- It is a question, as `find` is: in plain code it is asked right away, and in a rule, query, or `find` it is asked for each answer, with the values the answer bound in place. (`assuming-in-rule` is `asked-per-answer`)
- `["assuming", change, ..., expression]` gives what the expression gives in the database as it would be after the changes, each a `fact` or `retract` form made in order as that form makes it, with keys, merges, and invariants, and leaves the database as it is. (`assuming-leaves-database` is `unchanged`)
- `assuming` runs in the compiled evaluator as the other forms that ask the database do: as an Effect, in the scope it was written in, with its changes and its expression evaluated by the evaluator that runs it. The generated programs that check the compiled evaluator against the interpreter it replaced, and plain code against code that waits for a logic variable, now ask questions with `assuming` too, adding a link and taking one away before a `find`. (`compiled-assuming` is `same-as-interpreter`)
- A schema whose lambda asks about a value that is no smaller, such as a list that holds the value, is refused, since its check need not end. (`schema-asks-itself-larger-value` is `error`, was `out-of-memory`)
- A schema that fits exactly the values that do not fit it, such as ['schema', 'Odd', ['lambda', ['v'], ['not', ['schema/fits?', 'v', 'Odd']]]], fails, and says that whether the value fits has no answer. (`schema-asks-itself-no-consistent-answer` is `error`, was `out-of-memory`)
- A schema whose lambda asks about the schema again no longer runs Lion out of memory. One that only asks the same question again, such as ['schema', 'Same', ['lambda', ['v'], ['schema/fits?', 'v', 'Same']]], gets the least answer that agrees with itself: it does not fit. (`schema-asks-itself-same-value` is `no-fit`, was `out-of-memory`)
- A schema whose lambda asks about a smaller value, such as a list's tail, is checked as any schema is, so a schema that asks under not whether a list's tail fits it fits the lists of even length. (`schema-asks-itself-smaller-value` is `fits`)

### Fixed

- The test helper `attempt` reports a function in a program's value as Lion writes it, wherever it stands: `"even"` for the relation `even`, `["lambda", ["n"], "n"]` for a lambda. A program that ended in a declaration, such as `["begin", ["relation", "even", ["n"]]]`, made it throw `"undefined" is not valid JSON`, which failed the `why` property about rules that ask no question once in about 300 runs, and a function inside a list or a record was reported as `null` or left out. A property checks that two programs are reported the same only when their values are the same. (`test-report-function-value` is `written-as-lion`, was `error`)
- Two rows of a keyed aggregated relation that differ in a column that does not merge are now an error, as two rows rules give one key always were. (`keyed-aggregated-rows-differ` is `error`, was `both-held`)
- Each agent works in a folder of its own outside the repository, inside a folder that cannot be listed, and its prompts and transcripts are written straight to `out/skill-eval/<run>/`, which it never sees. (`skill-eval-agent-folder` is `outside-repository`, was `inside-repository`)
- The REPL's program graph shows an `assuming` form as a logic node that asks as if the database had changed, with the number of its changes. Without it the REPL did not type-check once `assuming` was a special form. (`repl-graph-assuming` is `logic-node`, was `type-error`)

### Decided

- Each class of values a key or an equality rule makes one is written as its least member, by one order (`merge-survivor`: `least-member-fewest-nested-then-first-sorted` over `pairwise-describes-then-first-sorted`, `first-sorted-alone`)
- Every research run adds to one Lion model in `research/`. (`research-layout`: `one-shared-model` over `folder-per-run`, `ignored-out-folder`)
- A lambda's body is compiled the first time the lambda is called and kept with the lambda; nothing else keeps compiled code, and the forms that read or change the logic database evaluate their parts as they run. (`compiled-code-lifetime`: `kept-by-lambda` over `cache-by-expression`)
- A call between Lion functions uses the JavaScript stack, and a call more than 100 calls deep starts again at the bottom of the stack, in Effect's run loop. (`deep-calls`: `unwind-to-run-loop` over `continuation-passing`, `generators`, `trampoline-per-tail-call`)
- An expression is compiled to a JavaScript function of the scope it runs in, and a special form is told apart when its expression is compiled, not each time it runs. (`evaluator-design`: `compile-to-direct-style` over `match-dispatch`, was `match-dispatch`)
- A scope holds its bindings in a `Map` that compiled code reads and writes right away. (`scope-bindings-store`: `map` over `ref`)
- On its own, a record or list of schemas leaves nothing; `["schema/open", record]` leaves anything; `["schema/open", record, rest]` leaves what fits `rest`. (`rest-of-a-record`: `schema-open-second-argument` over `reserved-key`, `open-by-default`, `allof-skips-keys`)
- A group whose rules invent into a relation with a key or beside other rules, or make values one, is followed a component of values at a time (`rule-that-invents-on-change`: `follow-a-component-at-a-time` over `follow-where-it-invents-alone`, `derive-again-every-case`, `track-rounds-per-row`, `skolem-for-keyed-relations`, `weighted-rows`)
- Each schema a library function checks its arguments with is compiled, once, into a guard: a plain JavaScript test that is true for exactly the values the schema's decoder accepts. (`library-argument-checks`: `guard-then-decoder` over `decoder-every-call`, `effect-jit-compiler`, `hand-written-checks`)
- A keyed relation can declare `"merge"`: for columns outside the key, the lattice aggregate that joins the values a key gets, `aggregate/min`, `aggregate/max`, `aggregate/shortest`, or `aggregate/union`. (`merging-what-a-key-gets`: `lattice-merge-option` over `facts-for-every-aggregate`, `written-merge-function`, `merge-on-write`)
- Each agent works in a folder of its own outside the repository, inside a folder that cannot be listed, and its prompts and transcripts are written straight to `out/skill-eval/<run>/`, which it never sees. (`skill-eval-agent-folder`: `outside-repository` over `inside-repository`)
- `["assuming", change, ..., expression]` gives what the expression gives in the database as it would be after the changes, each a `fact` or `retract` form made in order as that form makes it, with keys, merges, and invariants, and leaves the database as it is. (`asking-what-would-hold`: `assuming-question` over `binding-assumption`, `additions-only`, `copy-outside`)
- Asked about again for a smaller value, one that holds fewer values, counting each character of a string, it is checked as any schema is. Asked about again for any other value, the check is refused. (`schema-asks-about-another-value`: `only-smaller-values` over `any-value-but-an-equal-one`)
- Asked about again for the same value, it answers what the check under way assumes, which is first that the value does not fit. (`schema-asks-about-itself`: `least-consistent-answer-else-no-answer` over `always-error`, `does-not-fit`, `third-truth-value`)

### Rules

- a value the group did not invent, else the unknown that nests the fewest unknowns, else the one that sorts first (rule `least-member-survives-a-merge`)
- A run starts from what the model holds, reuses its names for features, techniques, sources, and candidates, and gives its own ids a prefix. (rule `research-runs-extend-shared-model`)
- A call in tail position keeps nothing of the call it is in, so a loop of 1,000,000 tail calls takes 0.5 s and 9 MB, and a call that is not in tail position can still nest as deep as memory allows. (rule `tail-calls-keep-nothing`)
- A record of schemas names some keys of a value, and a list of schemas its first items. What it leaves over is the rest: the record of the other keys, or the list of the items after. (rule `rest-of-a-record-or-list`)
- Arguments the guard accepts go to the function as they are, and any others go through the decoder, so every error and every message is the decoder's, as before. (rule `library-argument-errors-are-the-decoders`)
- A relation whose rules aggregate merges what each key gets with those aggregates, as one that declares a merge does: the other columns of the head are the key, and every fact of the relation and every rule with a plain head for it gives a candidate, whatever order they come in. (rule `a-key-holds-the-join-of-its-candidates`)
- Each agent works in a folder of its own outside the repository, inside a folder that cannot be listed, and its prompts and transcripts are written straight to `out/skill-eval/<run>/`, which it never sees. (rule `skill-eval-isolated-runs`)
- A rule or invariant form there is held, never made: its parts are evaluated as the form would evaluate them, and the database does not change, also when the question is refused. (rule `assuming-is-a-question`)
- A run names the candidates it weighs with `weighed_in`, new or shared, and its page asks only about those and about its own prefixed ids, so another run's beliefs and candidates do not change what it says. (rule `research-pages-keep-to-their-run`)
- A candidate that adds something Lion lacks, but needs a condition Lion lacks too, is `worth adopting in part`; one that adds nothing and needs such a condition `has nothing in Lion to work on`. Only `worth adopting` is ranked. (rule `research-verdict-in-part`)
- A named schema being checked is known to every check its shape starts, through a predicate too. (rule `schema-asks-about-itself`)
- After you commit a change with its pages, run `bun run history write` again and commit what it changed on its own, as `docs: write the project record's pages for <commit>`. Then run it once more and `git commit --amend --no-edit` that pages commit: an amend replaces the commit instead of adding one, so the count now includes it. (rule `pages-count-their-commit`)

## 2026-11-17 — @lionlang/core 0.13.0, @lionlang/cli 0.12.0

`why` explains a fact that does not hold as well as one that does: each rule that could have given it, the goals of its body that held, and what stopped it. It returned `null` before, so this is a breaking change. It marks with `"assumed": true` what could change once an unknown is known, and it shows the same derivation whatever order the facts arrived in. The lion-reasoning skill records what each experiment was expected to fix, and what is carried to a new score.

### Added

- A group of relations that a change to facts was followed through forgets how its rows came about, and the first `why` that needs them derives the group again beside the model. (`why-derives-followed-group-again` added)
- The lion-reasoning skill records what each experiment was expected to fix, and says whether it did. A direction's notes list the problems it `addresses`, and a belief that `tries` a direction, such as `{"id": "collapse-1", "tries": "collapse", "predicts": [{"question": {"case": "double spaces", "suite": "titles"}, "value": "pass"}]}`, is what one experiment of it was expected to do, judged in the version it was recorded in and no other. Where it predicts a problem's question otherwise than the problem shows, the experiment `targets` the problem, which is `fixed` or `unfixed` by what that version answers. `carried` lists the directions whose experiments say nothing about a score, because none ran it with an earlier version to beat, as when the same eval is run under another model or a new benchmark arrives. Three standing questions ask what each experiment was expected to fix and whether it did, which problems still show though every direction that addresses them is set aside, and which directions are carried to a score. `bun run skill-eval` prints the models that ran each run, as `models: claude-opus-5-5`, so a run's model can be part of the score's question. (`reasoning-belief-tries` added; `reasoning-carried` added; `reasoning-direction-addresses` added; `reasoning-fixed` added; `reasoning-targets` added; `reasoning-unfixed` added)

### Changed

- `["why", ["mother", "ada", "fay"]]` is not when ada's mother is eve (`why-absence-reads-no-unknown` is `not-assumed`)
- `["why", ["mother", "ben", "eve"]]` is marked `assumed` when ben's mother is unknown (`why-absence-reads-unknown` is `assumed`)
- Every `absent`, asked as it stands, is `false`, as every `check` is `true`. (`why-absent-evaluated` is `false`)
- An absent relation call is explained in turn, once in a tree (`why-absent-goal-repeated` is `told-once`)
- An unknown in a premise is written as its `some` form, which evaluates to the unknown; it was written as a quoted list. (`why-check-with-unknown-evaluated` is `true`, was `false`)
- Where several rows fit a goal with a variable of its own, the row shown is the one that sorts first (`why-matched-row-of-several` is `sorts-first`, was `added-first`)
- When a rule's whole body holds, its `but` is the row the rule gave instead. (`why-rule-gave-another-row` is `row-explained`)
- A `not` stops a rule because what it denies holds, so its `but` is that fact or check. (`why-stopped-by-not` is `what-it-denies`)
- The matches of an aggregated row are listed in the order of their answers, not the order they were found in. (`why-aggregated-matches-order` is `answer-order`, was `found-order`)
- A path is explained by its shortest chain of edges, the one that takes the least node at each step: once `["edge", "a", "c"]` is added, `["why", ["path", "a", "c"]]` rests on that edge. (`why-row-after-shorter-way` is `shorter-way`, was `derived-before`)
- Rules run in the order they were added, round after round from the facts up, and of the answers of the first rule that gives the row, the least explains it, comparing the values its body binds in the order it binds them. (`why-row-of-tied-answers` is `least-answer`, was `added-first`)
- A `fact` form that repeats a key of a keyed relation writes only its last row for that key, so a form whose last rows hold already changes nothing: `["fact", ["order", 1, "open"], ["order", 1, "shipped"]]` leaves a database that holds `["order", 1, "shipped"]` exactly as it was. It used to replace that row and put it back at the end, which moved it in the snapshot and in the order unordered answers come in. (`keyed-fact-form-last-row-holds` is `changes-nothing`, was `moves-row`)
- After the score is run on the earlier versions, `improved` and `paid_off` say whether each change helps there, and the direction is carried no longer. (`reasoning-carried-direction-after-earlier-run` is `judged`)
- A direction `addresses` the problems its notes list and the problems its experiments target. (`reasoning-direction-addresses` is `listed-and-targeted`)
- The lion-reasoning skill records what each experiment was expected to fix, and says whether it did. A direction's notes list the problems it `addresses`, and a belief that `tries` a direction, such as `{"id": "collapse-1", "tries": "collapse", "predicts": [{"question": {"case": "double spaces", "suite": "titles"}, "value": "pass"}]}`, is what one experiment of it was expected to do, judged in the version it was recorded in and no other. Where it predicts a problem's question otherwise than the problem shows, the experiment `targets` the problem, which is `fixed` or `unfixed` by what that version answers. `carried` lists the directions whose experiments say nothing about a score, because none ran it with an earlier version to beat, as when the same eval is run under another model or a new benchmark arrives. Three standing questions ask what each experiment was expected to fix and whether it did, which problems still show though every direction that addresses them is set aside, and which directions are carried to a score. `bun run skill-eval` prints the models that ran each run, as `models: claude-opus-5-5`, so a run's model can be part of the score's question. (`reasoning-direction-on-unrun-score` is `carried`; `reasoning-expectation-after-files-put-back` is `still-borne-out`, was `contradicted`; `reasoning-experiment-predicted-fixes` is `fixed-or-unfixed-by-its-version`; `reasoning-standing-questions-experiments` is `expected-fixes-open-problems-and-carried`; `skill-eval-run-models` is `printed`)
- Patience is counted as before, over every score. (`reasoning-patience-across-scores` is `counted-over-every-score`)

### Decided

- CI runs the tests with `bun run test --concurrency=1`, one package at a time, so a suite has the runner's cores to itself. (`ci-test-concurrency`: `one-package-at-a-time` over `every-package-at-once`)
- `why` tells how a goal holds or why it does not, by one rule in which `not` changes sides. (`why-fact-not-holding`: `absent-tree` over `null`)
- A row that several answers give is explained the way a database that derives it from the facts as they now are finds it first. (`why-derivation-shown`: `found-first-by-new-derivation` over `first-kept`)
- `carried` lists each direction that has experiments, with each score that none of them ran in a version with an earlier version to beat. (`reasoning-direction-record-across-scores`: `carried-list` over `patience-per-score`, `setting-field`)
- it is judged in the version it was formed in and no other (`reasoning-experiment-expectation-judging`: `its-own-version` over `from-its-version-on`, `attempt-notes`, `predictions-on-the-direction`)

### Rules

- CI runs the tests with `bun run test --concurrency=1`, one package at a time (rule `ci-tests-one-package-at-a-time`)
- Every package's `vitest.config.ts` gives a test 120 seconds (rule `test-time-limit-two-minutes`)
- `"assumed": true` marks what could change once an unknown is known (rule `why-marks-assumptions`)
- `why` tells how a goal holds or why it does not, by one rule in which `not` changes sides. (rule `why-tells-both-sides`)
- A row that several answers give is explained the way a database that derives it from the facts as they now are finds it first. Rules run in the order they were added, round after round from the facts up, and of the answers of the first rule that gives the row, the least explains it, comparing the values its body binds in the order it binds them. (rule `why-tells-derivation-found-first`)
- `carried` lists each direction that has experiments, with each score that none of them ran in a version with an earlier version to beat. (rule `direction-record-speaks-for-its-scores`)
- Such a belief is what one experiment of the direction was expected to do, and it is judged in the version it was formed in and no other. Where it predicts a problem's question otherwise than the problem shows, that experiment `targets` the problem; the problem is `fixed` there when the version answers as predicted, and `unfixed` when it answers otherwise. (rule `experiment-expectation-in-its-version`)

## 2026-11-17 — @lionlang/core 0.12.0, @lionlang/cli 0.11.0

The logic layer grows with its facts. What rules derive is kept and follows every change to the facts and the rules, in a full database and in a goal-directed query, instead of being derived again from nothing. A join runs in batches and keeps far less in memory, and a load under invariants or schemas no longer reads every row again for each form.

### Added

- `bun run scale` times workloads at growing sizes, 1,000 to 100,000 facts and more, on the working tree or beside a commit with `--against main`, and says how each time grows: `n^1.0` grows as the facts do, and `n^2.0` as their square. It covers loading facts one form at a time and many, with invariants, schemas, and keys; asking every row and one; rules that join, aggregate, negate, and recurse; and changing a fact and asking again. Each run is a process of its own, and reports the memory it added at its peak and what the database keeps. (`bun-run-scale` added)
- `bun run mutate --in logic/model.ts,logic/database.ts` runs only the mutations in files whose paths hold one of the words, so a change can run the mutations of the files it touched instead of the whole list. (`mutate-in-option` added)
- `bun run mutate` breaks copies of the working tree under `out/metrics/`, as it stood when the run began, four at a time, so the code can be edited while it runs. `--jobs` says how many run at once. (`mutate-in-copies` added)
- `bun run compare main --docs` runs every program the docs show, the fenced `json` blocks of the README files, `AGENTS.md`, the guidelines in `guidelines/`, and each skill's `SKILL.md` and `references/`, on a commit and on the working tree, and lists the ones that give something else: the documented results a change made untrue. (`compare-docs-option` added)
- Each guideline is a source of the project record, so the 85 claims that quoted `AGENTS.md` quote the file their sentence is in now (`guidelines-folder` added)
- its opening and the sections that describe the language, from Basic Usage to Host Interop, word for word (`package-readme-writer` added)

### Changed

- so answer order now depends on the changes a database has seen (`answer-order-after-fact-changes` is `depends-on-changes`)
- A failed assertion or invariant shows as its example the first answer in sort order, not the first one found, so the message does not depend on the order the answers were found in. (`assertion-example-answer` is `first-in-sort-order`, was `first-found`)
- A change an invariant refuses forgets what was derived (`derived-rows-after-refused-change` is `derived-again`)
- A `fact` or `retract` form no longer copies the relations it writes to when the database holds an invariant, a relation's `schema` among them: when the change is refused it takes back each row it added or removed, and every fact is where it was. Loading 100,000 facts one form at a time into a relation with a schema takes 1.5 seconds; before, it did not finish in a minute, and the time grew with the square of the facts. (`fact-form-cost-under-invariants` is `grows-with-the-change`, was `grows-with-the-relation`)
- A `rule` form keeps the rows of the relations whose rules did not change, a change an invariant refuses leaves what was derived in place, and a goal-directed query keeps what it derived and follows changes. With a million facts, asking every row of a rule after a fact was refused takes 0.23 seconds, where it took 2.7, and after a rule was added to another relation 0.24 seconds, where it took 1.9. (`derived-rows-after-refused-change` is `kept`, was `derived-again`)
- A `rule` form derives again the relations whose rules changed and keeps the rows of every other (`derived-rows-after-rule-change` is `kept-where-rules-did-not-change`, was `derived-again`)
- A change is followed through every relation goal of a rule, wherever it stands: in a `find`, in a goal used as a value, and in a rule's head, as well as in its body and under `not`. Counting with zeros, `["aggregate/count", ["find", "?id", ["order", "?id", "?c", "?_"]]]` in a head, no longer starts over at each change: with a million facts, adding a fact and asking every count takes 13 milliseconds, where it took 0.26 seconds. (`find-over-changed-relation-after-change` is `followed`, was `derived-again`)
- A goal-directed query keeps what it derived and follows changes, for up to 32 queries (`goal-directed-rows-after-fact-change` is `followed`, was `derived-again`)
- An aggregate that recurses, such as shortest distances by `aggregate/min`, goes on from the rows it has when facts change. With 100,000 roads, adding a road and asking every distance takes 15 milliseconds, where it took 0.7 seconds. (`recursive-aggregate-after-fact-change` is `followed`, was `derived-again`)

### Decided

- A rule body hands its answers on a batch at a time (`answers-of-a-rule-body`: `batches` over `every-answer-at-once`, `one-answer-at-a-time`)
- A failed assertion shows as its example the first answer in sort order (`assertion-example-answer`: `first-in-sort-order` over `first-found`)
- A database keeps what it derived when facts change, and follows the change through it (`derived-rows-on-fact-change`: `follow-the-change` over `forget-everything`, `forget-readers-of-what-changed`, `count-answers-per-row`)
- A change is undone by taking back each row it added or removed, in place, instead of copying the relation first (`undoing-a-change-to-facts`: `take-back-each-row` over `copy-the-relation`)
- A change an invariant refuses is followed back (`derived-rows-on-refused-change`: `follow-back` over `forget-everything`, `log-the-upkeep`)
- A `rule` form derives again the relations whose rules changed and keeps the rows of every other (`derived-rows-on-rule-change`: `derive-again-what-changed` over `forget-everything`)
- A rule that invents is followed where it invents alone, into a relation with no key (`rule-that-invents-on-change`: `follow-where-it-invents-alone` over `follow-every-case`, `derive-again-every-case`)
- A relation that cannot be followed is derived again and compared with what it was, when something has read it since the last change; then the relations that read it are followed (`unfollowable-relation-on-change`: `derive-again-and-compare-once-read` over `forget-with-readers`, `always-derive-again`)
- The mutation check breaks copies of the working tree, four at a time (`where-mutations-run`: `copies-of-the-working-tree` over `the-working-tree`)
- `AGENTS.md` names every one with its main rule and when to read it (`agent-instructions-layout`: `index-with-guidelines` over `one-file`, `imported-guidelines`)
- The docs build, `bun run --cwd packages/site-runtime docs`, writes `packages/core/README.md` from `README.md` (`package-readme-source`: `written-from-readme` over `kept-by-hand`, `pointer-to-repository`)

### Rules

- A database keeps what it derived when facts change, and follows the change through it (rule `derived-rows-follow-fact-changes`)
- A `retract` of facts and rules is one change, and a rule that asks `assuming` is derived again and compared after a change, as one that holds a function from the host is. (rule `unfollowed-relations-derived-again`)
- `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md` stay under 200 lines each. (rule `agent-instructions-under-200-lines`)
- `guidelines/documentation.md` and `CLAUDE.md` state the documentation procedure to run after every change, in order: run the docs' programs, search every place that describes Lion for sentences the change made untrue, give each new feature its example, rebuild the website's docs, record the change with the claims that quote reworded sentences, and say what the first two steps found. The package README, both skills' references, and the website's guarantees still described derived rows as recomputed or kept only until a change, and now say that they follow changes. (rule `documentation-procedure`)
- The documentation procedure no longer asks for every edit to be made in both files. (rule `edit-both-readmes` withdrawn)
- Each practice has a guideline in `guidelines/`, and `AGENTS.md` names every one with its main rule and when to read it. (rule `every-guideline-named-in-agents-md`)
- The site-runtime tests fail when the file is not what the README gives. (rule `package-readme-written-from-readme`)

## 2026-11-16 — @lionlang/core 0.11.0, @lionlang/cli 0.10.0

Rules that say something exists without saying what it is. A rule can give every person a mother nobody has named, and the value it invents is ordinary Lion data that a program can write, store, compare, and look up. Keys and equality rules make two such values one, and a goal-directed query shares what full evaluation has derived.

### Breaking

- `exists` and `some` are names of special forms, so a relation, a `define`, or a lambda parameter can no longer be named `"exists"` or `"some"`. (`name-exists-or-some` is `error`, was `allowed`)
- It is a breaking change: `why` no longer returns `null` for a fact that does not hold (`why-goal-not-holding` is `absent-tree`, was `null`)

### Added

- `exists` in a rule head says the head holds for values nobody has given, and the rule invents them: `["rule", ["exists", ["?m"], ["mother", "?c", "?m"]], ["person", "?c"]]` gives every person a mother. A rule invents only for an answer whose head does not hold already, so with `["mother", "ada", "eve"]` it invents nothing for ada. Rules that invent wait until every rule that invents nothing has finished; then every rule that invents, and whose head does not hold, invents, all at once; the other rules run on what was invented; and so on until nothing more is invented. So the result does not depend on the order rules or facts were added in. A rule that invents cannot aggregate: `... both aggregates and lists variables in "exists". A rule that invents values cannot aggregate.` (`existential-rules` added; `exists` added)
- A rule head can be an `and` of relation calls, which adds a row to each: `["rule", ["and", ["p", "?x"], ["q", "?x"]], ["r", "?x"]]`. Under `exists`, one unknown is in every row: `["exists", ["?m"], ["and", ["mother", "?c", "?m"], ["female", "?m"]]]`. (`rule-head-and` added)
- An invented value is an unknown, written `["some", "?m", ["mother", "ben", "?m"]]`: the value `?m` stands for in a row `["mother", "ben", ?m]`. The form is ordinary Lion, and evaluating it gives the same unknown, so a program can write one, put it in a fact (`["fact", ["tall", ["some", "?m", ["mother", "ben", "?m"]]]]`), compare it, or look it up. Inside a rule, query, or `find`, `some` waits for the variables the rule binds, and every other variable of its goal is its own. With several variables in `exists`, each gets its own unknown, and its `some` mentions the others. (`some` added)
- `["value/unknown?", x]` is true for an unknown, so `["not", ["value/unknown?", "?m"]]` keeps only known answers. `value/object?` is false for one. (`value/unknown?` added)
- `why` explains a row by an answer that gives it as it is, when one does, so after a key makes unknowns equal every row still rests only on rows explained before it. A row that a rule which invents gives only because a key or an equality rule made its unknown equal to another value ends with a new kind of premise, `{"same": [unknown, value]}`. (`why-same-premise` added)
- Equality rules: a rule's head can be `["=", a, b]`, which says the two are one value whenever the body holds. While Lion derives the relations its body reads, an unknown a rule invented there takes the value on the other side, by the rule keys follow, so `["rule", ["=", "?m1", "?m2"], ["twin", "?a", "?b"], ["mother", "?a", "?m1"], ["mother", "?b", "?m2"]]` gives twins one mother. The rule holds as the invariant `["and", goal, ..., ["not", ["=", a, b]]]` too, so a rule that does not hold is not added and a change that leaves two different known values under it is refused, in an invariant's words. Lion follows each invented value through the columns of the rules, and the relations of a rule that invents an unknown an equality rule can meet are derived together with every relation the equality rule reads, so the unknown can pass through any number of derived relations first: with `["rule", ["listed", "?c", "?m"], ["mother", "?c", "?m"]]`, the rule `["rule", ["=", "?m", "?n"], ["listed", "?c", "?m"], ["birth", "?c", "?n"]]` makes ben's unknown mother the one his birth record names. A question about those relations, such as a goal under `not`, would go through recursion, and is refused when the rule is added, whatever the facts, with the message any such question gets: `Rule [...] asks about "private", which depends on "listed". A question, such as a goal under "not", cannot go through recursion.` A question about columns no invented value reaches is allowed. A head can join relation calls and `=` with `and`. (`equality-rules` added; `invented-value-flow-tracking` added)
- `["string/slice", text, start, end]` and `["array/slice", list, start, end]` cut a part out of text or a list, as JavaScript's `slice` does: a negative position counts from the end, a position past either end stops there, and without `end` the part runs to the end. `["string/slice", "lionlang", 0, 4]` is `"lion"`, and the two parts of a cut at any position join back into the whole. Like every library function, each waits for a logic variable and refuses a call with another number of arguments. (`array/slice` added; `string/slice` added)
- `bun run history check` fails on a finding of the model that is not listed in `history/research/known.json` or accepted with a reason; the record's tests run it, so CI does. `bun run history ask` asks the model anything, such as `["query", ["reversed", "?topic", "?before", "?after"]]` for the decisions the project made one way and later another. A decision in `history/decisions/` can carry `"claims"` quoted from its own words, so the model checks what it settles against every rule and behavior. (`bun-run-history-ask` added; `history-research-reversed` added)
- The project record. `history/` keeps Lion's changes, releases, and design decisions as a Lion model of the git history. Entries are plain JSON files; every commit, parent, and tag, and the commit that added each file, are facts; and rules derive the rest, such as the release that shipped each change, which is the oldest release whose commit reaches every commit that made it. `CHANGELOG.md` and the new `DECISIONS.md` are written from the record by Lion expressions, with `bun run history write`. `bun run history check` fails on a `feat` or `fix` commit that no entry records, a quote that is not in the commit message it cites, a breaking change that follows from no decision, a version that does not rise, a tag on the wrong commit, or a page that is not what the record writes, and the tests and CI run it. `bun run history ask` asks the record anything. (`bun-run-history-check` added; `project-record` added)
- a change is recorded in `history/changes/`, and `bun run history write` writes the page (`bun-run-history-write` added)
- A decision can say in the research's terms what it settles, with `"claims"` quoted from its own words, so it is checked against every rule and behavior as soon as it is written. (`decision-claims` added)
- Rules in `history/core.json` derive the rest (`history-core-json` added)
- `CHANGELOG.md` and `DECISIONS.md` are computed by the Lion expressions in `history/outputs.json`, and the record's tests fail when either file is not what the record writes (`history-outputs-json` added)
- `makeTypeSafeBindings` accepts the TypeSafe SDK client configuration and an optional namespace. (`makeTypeSafeBindings` added)
- A lookup on a research page can lead with an answer in words: `"answer"` is a `lambda` like its `query`, the page shows the sentence it gives for what the reader chose, and folds the rows under "Show the evidence". A lookup with more than twelve options is a box that finds them as the reader types, not a long list. `page-check.json` asks the answer with the example. (`research-lookup-answer` added)
- `bun run history research` builds the model and the page `docs/research.html`, and fails on a finding in neither list and on a listed finding the model no longer finds (`research-model` added)
- probes that run a program on Lion at HEAD are sources too (`research-probes` added)
- `researches-recent-outage` researches a live event on the web. (`researches-recent-outage` added)
- The lion-reasoning skill keeps the record of an experiment loop, in the manner of autoresearch. Notes declare `scores`, numbers to make better, such as `{"question": {"comparisons": "bench.mjs"}, "better": "lower"}`, and `directions`, the ideas to try; an observation that `tries` a direction is an experiment of it. What a version is sure to give is its worst run: the `best` is the best of those, and a version has `improved` a score when its worst run beats the best that any earlier version was sure to give, so `improved`, `best`, `score_in`, and `regressed` follow the evidence and not one lucky run; runs of a score that differ are its spread rather than a dispute. A direction pays off when its experiment improves a score, and is set aside after as many misses in a row as its `patience`, 3 unless it says. The standing question "Where should the next experiment go?" lists the live directions, those that paid off most first. (`reasoning-best` added; `reasoning-improved` added; `reasoning-notes-directions` added; `reasoning-notes-scores` added; `reasoning-observation-tries` added; `reasoning-regressed` added; `reasoning-score-in` added)
- A lion-reasoning score can list what it `requires`, such as `{"question": {"failing tests": "test.mjs"}, "value": 0}`: a version where that is unanswered or answered otherwise has no score, and `unmet` lists it, so a change that breaks the tests never becomes the best. A direction can list what it `needs`; it waits while that is unanswered or not yet decided by whoever `ask` names, and is `ruled_out` once it is answered otherwise, so the user's answer prunes the directions. (`reasoning-direction-needs` added; `reasoning-ruled-out` added; `reasoning-score-requires` added; `reasoning-unmet` added)
- Both skills use the one rule and the same names: `paid_off`, `miss`, `exhausted`, `live`, `progress` (`reasoning-exhausted` added; `reasoning-live` added; `reasoning-miss` added; `reasoning-paid-off` added; `reasoning-progress` added; `research-exhausted` added; `research-live` added; `research-miss` added; `research-paid-off` added; `research-progress` added)
- The reasoning skill now keeps the record of an experiment loop (`reasoning-experiment-loop` added)
- The lion-research skill records leads, the lines of inquiry a research run follows: `"leads": [{"id": "outside-warehouses", "statement": "…", "pursues": [{"warehouses": "tarnwell"}], "patience": 2}]`, and `"for": ["outside-warehouses"]` on each source read to follow one. A source pays off for a lead when it says something first-hand the lead covers; a lead is `done` when every question it pursues has an answer two publishers give first-hand, waits while the reader has not chosen a sense of one, and is set aside after as many sources in a row as its patience gave it nothing. The standing questions ask where the next search should go and how each lead ended, and the page's "How solid is it" shows the reader "What was looked for". (`research-lead-done` added; `research-leads` added; `research-source-for` added)
